Ising anyons in the S ​ U ​ ( 2 ) 2 SU(2)_{2} Chern–Simons theory
Artem Belov b,c, Andrey Morozov a,b,c

Abstract
The present work is motivated by the statement that the Ising minimal model ℳ ⁡ ( 4 , 3 ) \mathcal{M}(4,3) is equivalent, at the level of observables, to the S ​ U ​ ( 2 ) 2 SU(2)_{2} Chern–Simons theory. At first glance, however, these two theories appear to differ substantially. For instance, the number of irreducible highest-weight representations does not match the number of Ising anyons. For tensor products of low degree, these discrepancies are examined in this work. While representation structure differs, it does not affect the observables underlying topological quantum computation algorithms.



ITEP/TH - 25/26

IITP/TH - 23/26

MIPT/TH 21/26

a
ITEP, Moscow, 123182, Russia

b
Institute for Information Transmission Problems, Moscow 127994, Russia

c
Moscow Institute of Physics and Technology, Dolgoprudny 141701, Russia

1
Introduction

In the 1980s, researchers encountered a computational problem: simulating quantum systems on classical computers turned out to be inefficient. This led to the idea that the simulation of quantum processes should be based on quantum computers
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
. Interest in this subject increased sharply after the appearance of papers on a polynomial-time algorithm for integer factorization
[
4
]
and a fast database search algorithm
[
5
]
. A quantum computer differs substantially from a classical one. Unlike a classical bit of information, which can be in only one of two states, a quantum bit, or qubit
[
6
]
, can be in a superposition of states. However, it soon became clear that such algorithms are difficult to implement because of quantum decoherence
[
7
,
8
]
. One solution to this problem was provided by quantum error-correction methods
[
9
,
10
]
.

A fundamentally new approach emerged with the development of topological quantum computation
[
11
]
. Such computations are based on anyons
[
12
]
—particles in
2+1
-dimensional spacetime that realize fractional statistics
[
13
]
. It was shown that particles interacting through a Chern–Simons field possess fractional statistics. Recall that Chern–Simons theory has a topologically invariant action. Thus, anyons turned out to be closely connected with Chern–Simons theory. This gave rise to a branch of topological quantum algorithms described by observables in Chern–Simons theory, namely Wilson loops. Direct computation of Wilson loops is itself a difficult problem. However, it was shown that in the case of
SU(2)
gauge symmetry in the Chern–Simons action, the expectation value of a Wilson loop can be expressed in terms of the Jones polynomial of the knot along which the contour of integration in the Wilson loop is taken
[
14
]
. The Jones polynomial
itself can be computed rather efficiently using the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
, which involves the representation theory of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})

[
18
]
. Thus, the construction of topological quantum algorithms reduces to the action of operators of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
on a certain vector space of anyon states. In the quantum-algebraic framework, the key objects for constructing topological quantum computation are therefore the tensor-product decomposition rules into irreducible components, the Racah matrix, and the
\mathcal{R}
-matrix. These three objects are precisely what is used to form topological quantum computations in the language of quantum algebras
[
19
,
20
]
.

Another possible way to study observables in Chern–Simons theory is to use conformal field theory
[
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
]
. A constant-time slice of a Wilson loop in
(2+1)
-dimensional spacetime is described by a conformal field theory. The intersection of the Wilson loop, that is, of the knot along which the integration contour runs, with a constant-time plane gives a set of points whose interactions are described by correlation functions of a two-dimensional conformal field theory. Thus, the formalism of two-dimensional conformal field theory is also used to describe topological quantum algorithms. The key objects used in the construction of topological quantum algorithms in this setting are the fusion rules, the braiding matrix, and the fusion matrix. This set of three objects is sufficient
to construct a topological quantum algorithm within the framework of two-dimensional conformal field theory.

There is a statement that the minimal model
\mathcal{M}(4,3)
, also known as the Ising minimal model, corresponds to
SU(2)_{2}
Chern–Simons theory
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
. It is also claimed that the observables of
SU(2)_{2}
Chern–Simons theory are constructed using the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at
q=\exp(\pi i/4)
. The purpose of this paper is to compare the tensor-product decomposition rules of representations into direct sums, the Racah matrices, and the
\mathcal{R}
-matrices of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at
q=\exp(\pi i/4)
with the fusion rules, fusion matrix, and braiding rules of the Ising minimal model. These objects will exhibit differences and, at times, apparent contradictions. All such contradictions will be resolved.

fusion of anyons

rotation of anyons

change of basis in Hilbert space

U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
for
q=\exp(\pi i/4)

direct sum decomposition

\mathcal{R}-matrix

Racah matrix

\mathcal{M}(4,3)

fusion rules

braiding rules

fusion matrix

1.1
Ising minimal model

The minimal model
\mathcal{M}(4,3)
, also known as the Ising minimal model, is a two-dimensional conformal field theory
[
29
]
. Its central charge is
c=1/2
. In this minimal model, three fields form the holomorphic part. Their conformal dimensions are
0
,
1/2
, and
1/16
. The following notation is often used:

\begin{split}\mathbbm{1}&\leftrightarrow\phi_{(1,1)}\;\text{or}\;\phi_{(2,3)}\\
\sigma&\leftrightarrow\phi_{(2,2)}\;\text{or}\;\phi_{(1,2)}\\
\varepsilon\equiv\psi&\leftrightarrow\phi_{(2,1)}\;\text{or}\;\phi_{(1,3)},\end{split}

(1)

where
\phi_{(1,1)},\phi_{(2,3)},\phi_{(2,2)},\phi_{(1,2)},\phi_{(2,1)}
and
\phi_{(1,3)}
are primary fields. The Ising minimal model is also related to the critical Ising model. In particular,
\sigma
is the Ising spin field, that is, the continuum version of the lattice spin
\sigma_{i}
, while
\varepsilon
has the meaning of an energy-density field, that is, the continuum version of the interaction energy
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}
. However, in the context of topological quantum computation, one often writes
\psi
instead of
\varepsilon
. The field
\psi
is also called a Majorana fermion, since
\psi
is its own antiparticle (
3
) and the exchange of two such fields with each other (
5
) gives a factor of
(-1)
.

It is precisely these three fields,
\mathbbm{1}
(the vacuum),
\sigma
, and
\psi
, that are called Ising anyons. The corresponding OPEs for these fields are
[
30
]
:

\begin{split}\sigma(z)\sigma(0)&\sim z^{-1/8}[\mathbbm{1}+\dots]+z^{3/8}[\psi(0)+\dots],\\
\psi(z)\sigma(0)&\sim z^{-1/2}\sigma(0)+\dots,\\
\psi(z)\psi(0)&\sim z^{-1}\,\mathbbm{1}.\end{split}

(2)

Let us pay attention to the fields that appear on the right-hand side of equations (
2
). In the terminology of topological quantum computation, this information is written in the following form:

\begin{split}\sigma\times\sigma&=\mathbbm{1}+\psi,\\
\psi\times\sigma&=\sigma,\\
\psi\times\psi&=\mathbbm{1},\end{split}

(3)

and is often called the fusion rules. Keeping in mind that the fields
\mathbbm{1}
,
\sigma
, and
\psi
are understood as anyons, these rules are usually interpreted as the possible fusion channels of particles with one another. Thus, the symbol ”
\times
” on the left-hand side of the equality sign denotes fusion. On the right-hand side, all possible anyons that can result from the fusion are listed, separated by the symbol ”
+
”. The fusion rules (
3
) are part of a more general formula valid for minimal models
\mathcal{M}(p,p^{\prime})
. A graphical representation of the fusion rules can be seen in Figure
1
. Thus, for example, the fusion rule
\sigma\times\sigma=\mathbbm{1}+\psi
corresponds to Figures
1c
and
1d
.

\sigma
\psi
\sigma

(a)
\sigma\times\psi\to\sigma

\psi
\psi
\mathbbm{1}

(b)
\psi\times\psi\to\mathbbm{1}

\sigma
\sigma
\mathbbm{1}

(c)
\sigma\times\sigma\to\mathbbm{1}

\sigma
\sigma
\psi

(d)
\sigma\times\sigma\to\psi

Figure 1:
Graphical representation of the fusion rules (
3
).

The OPE (
2
) also gives us the braiding rules – the phases by which a vector in the Hilbert space changes under the exchange of two particles. Then the exchange (
z\to e^{i\pi}z
) of
\sigma\times\sigma
anyons gives
R_{\sigma\sigma}^{\mathbbm{1}}=e^{-i\pi/8}
in the case where the two
\sigma
anyons form the vacuum, or
R_{\sigma\sigma}^{\psi}=e^{3i\pi/8}
in the case where the two
\sigma
anyons form
\psi
. Thus, we obtain the braiding rules for the
\sigma\times\sigma
anyons:

R_{\sigma\sigma}=\begin{pmatrix}R_{\sigma\sigma}^{\mathbbm{1}}&0\\
0&R_{\sigma\sigma}^{\psi}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\pi/8}&0\\
0&e^{3i\pi/8}\end{pmatrix}=e^{-i\pi/8}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&e^{i\pi/2}\end{pmatrix}

(4)

The braiding rules for other pairs of anyons are not interesting in the context of observables, since they contribute at most to the overall phase of the system. That is, since in formula (
3
) the fusions
\sigma\times\psi
and
\psi\times\psi
each have only one fusion channel, their braiding matrices are as follows:

\begin{split}R_{\psi\psi}&=R^{\mathbbm{1}}_{\psi\psi}=e^{-i\pi}=-1,\\
R_{\psi\sigma}&=R^{\sigma}_{\psi\sigma}=e^{-i\pi/2}\end{split}

(5)

Therefore, the anyon
\psi
is often called a Majorana fermion. The operator
R_{\psi\sigma}
changes the basis on which it acts by exchanging the anyons
\psi
and
\sigma
.

Consider the four-point correlator of fields
\langle\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}\rangle
. Let us note that, in accordance with the fusion rule (
3
), there is freedom in choosing the order of fusion. For example, one can first fuse the last two anyons,
\langle\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4})\rangle
, or one can first fuse the second and third anyons,
\langle\sigma_{1}(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3})\sigma_{4}\rangle
. This leads to two different decompositions of the same correlator.
The correlator
\langle\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}\rangle
can be reduced by a conformal transformation to the computation of the correlator
\langle\sigma(\infty)\sigma(1)\sigma(z)\sigma(0)\rangle
. In this case, the fusion
\sigma(z)\sigma(0)
is called the
s
-channel, while the fusion
\sigma(1)\sigma(z)
is called the
t
-channel. The conformal blocks corresponding to the
s
-channel are

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbbm{1}}^{(s)}(z)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[z(1-z)]^{-1/8}\sqrt{1+\sqrt{1-z}},\\
\mathcal{F}_{\psi}^{(s)}(z)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[z(1-z)]^{-1/8}\sqrt{1-\sqrt{1-z}}.\end{split}

(6)

The chiral blocks corresponding to the
t
-channel are

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbbm{1}}^{(t)}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[z(1-z)]^{-1/8}\sqrt{1+\sqrt{z}}\\
\mathcal{F}_{\psi}^{(t)}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[z(1-z)]^{-1/8}\sqrt{1-\sqrt{z}}\end{split}

(7)

The correspondence between the chiral blocks of the
s
-channel and the
t
-channel is

\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{\psi}\end{pmatrix}=F_{\sigma}^{\sigma\sigma\sigma}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{\psi}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{\psi}\end{pmatrix}

(8)

The matrix
F_{\sigma}^{\sigma\sigma\sigma}
is called the fusion matrix. It is the transition matrix between bases in the Hilbert space, which are fixed by the choice of the order of fusion (Fig.
2
). The corresponding order of fusion is often encoded by diagrams, as shown on the left and on the right in Fig.
2
.

\mathcal{F}^{(s)}_{i}
\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{j}
\sigma
\sigma
\sigma
\sigma
i
F^{\tiny\sigma\sigma\sigma}_{\tiny\sigma}
\sigma
\sigma
\sigma
\sigma
j

Figure 2:
Fusion matrix
F^{\tiny\sigma\sigma\sigma}_{\tiny\sigma}
changes the decomposition of conformal blocks from the
s
-channel on the left to the
t
-channel on the right.

1.2
Chern–Simons theory

The description of topological quantum computation in terms of Chern–Simons theory is currently being actively developed
[
21
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
27
,
20
,
19
]
. Chern–Simons theory is a model of quantum field theory whose action

S[\mathcal{A}]=\frac{k}{4\pi}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\text{Tr}[\mathcal{A}\wedge d\mathcal{A}+\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}]

(9)

is topologically invariant. Here,
\mathcal{A}
denotes the gauge field,
k
is the Chern–Simons level, and
\mathcal{M}
is the manifold over which the integration is performed. Here, as usual,
\mathcal{M}=S^{3}
is considered. There is a special kind of observables in such a theory, called Wilson loop averages.

\langle W^{K}_{R}\rangle=\left\langle\text{Tr}_{R}\text{Pexp}\oint_{K}\mathcal{A}\right\rangle,

(10)

where,
K
is the closed contour of integration in 2+1 dimensional space (that is, a knot), and
R
is the representation of gauge groupe.

One possible way to compute a Wilson loop is to use the quantum algebra corresponding to the gauge group. In this paper, we consider
SU(2)
Chern–Simons theory; therefore, to compute the expectation value of a Wilson loop, one should use the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
.

(a)
3D representation of
5_{1}
knot

(Source: Katlas)

\hat{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{2}

(b)
5_{2}
knot with braid closure

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{3}\mathcal{R}_{3}\mathcal{R}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{2}^{-1}
\langle\varnothing\otimes\varnothing\to\varnothing\mid
\mid\varnothing\otimes\varnothing\to\varnothing\rangle

(c)
5_{2}
knot with plat closure

Figure 3:
The
5_{2}
knot from the Rolfsen table in different representations.

Let us discuss two possible ways of computing the average of a Wilson loop within the representation theory of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. Let the integration contour
K
in formula (
10
) form the knot
5_{2}
from the Rolfsen table (Fig.
3a
). Such a knot can be represented as a braid closure (Fig.
3b
) and as a plat closure (Fig.
3c
). In the case of a different integration contour, the number of strands may be different. The reasoning below naturally generalizes to a larger number of strands. Without loss of generality, assume that each line carries the fundamental representation. In the case of an
SU(N)
gauge group with
N\neq 2
, one would have to add arrows to the lines. These arrows would denote the fundamental and antifundamental representations, that is, an anyon and an anti-anyon. However, in the case of the
SU(2)
gauge group, this is not necessary, since the fundamental representation is antifundamental. From the point of view of representation theory, this means that in the decomposition of the tensor product

\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}=\varnothing\oplus\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}&\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}

(11)

into a direct sum, the trivial representation
\varnothing
appears.

1.2.1
Braid closure of
5_{2}
knot.

In the braid closure (Fig.
3b
), there is a braiding of three fundamental representations

\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\;.

(12)

The operators
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
act on vectors in this space. These are precisely the operators that exchange two representations, or equivalently two strands. The action of these operators on vectors in a tensor product of two representations is defined by the well-known formula

\mathcal{R}=q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n(n+1)/2}(1-q^{-2})^{n}}{[n]_{q}!}E^{n}\otimes F^{n},

(13)

where
q
is the deformation parameter of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
,
E
is the raising operator,
F
is the lowering operator, and
H
is the Cartan operator. Suppose that the operator
\mathcal{R}
acts on the first pair of fundamental representations, which decompose into the direct sum

\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}=\varnothing\oplus\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}&\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}\;.

(14)

Then the vectors from the decomposition
\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\to\varnothing
are eigenvectors of the operator
\mathcal{R}
with the same eigenvalue corresponding to this decomposition. The vectors from the decomposition
\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\to\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}&\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}
are also eigenvectors, but they have a different eigenvalue, the same for every vector in this decomposition. The vectors in a tensor product of arbitrary representations decomposed into a direct sum will always be eigenvectors of
\mathcal{R}
. The origin of this fact will be explained in Section
2
. Therefore, it is customary to regard
\mathcal{R}
as acting not on vectors inside a given representation, but on the representations themselves.

In order to write down the explicit form of the matrices
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
for the knot in the braid closure (Fig.
3b
), one must choose an order for decomposing the tensor product

\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule into a direct sum. There are two possible choices for the order of decomposition:

, the corresponding operator is

(20)

where
\mathfrak{d}_{i}
is the quantum dimension, defined as follows

\mathfrak{d}_{i}=\frac{q^{i}-q^{-i}}{q-q^{-1}}.

(21)

The origin of the quantum dimension in formula (
20
) is explained quite simply. The point is that in formula (
20
) one takes the quantum trace not over all representations, but over all vectors. The quantum trace itself consists of a sequence of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
-matrices, which encode the braiding, and the matrix
H
, which acts on all vectors. It is precisely the trace of the matrix
H
that gives the quantum dimension for the corresponding representations.

Let us also mention the fact that in the basis
\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes(\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt})\,
, the matrix
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is diagonal, while in the basis
(\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt})\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\,
, the matrix
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is diagonal. The transition between these bases is performed using the so-called Racah matrix, which can be computed directly by writing out the vectors for both bases.

1.2.2
Plat closure of
5_{2}
knot.

, and

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.60774pt}{-45.46524pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{76.82323pt}{-45.46524pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{39.67957pt}{-63.91806pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right]

(25)

Thus, in accordance with Fig.
3c
, the expectation value of the Wilson loop is equal to

\langle W^{K}_{R}\rangle=\langle\varnothing\otimes\varnothing\to\varnothing\mid\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\mid\varnothing\otimes\varnothing\to\varnothing\rangle.

(26)

These two methods of computing the Wilson loop must lead to equivalent answers. However, one has to be careful about how the tensor product of representations decomposes into a direct sum. Recall that, when computing the expectation value of a Wilson loop in this section, we are dealing with the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. Earlier, in formulas (
15
), (
18
), (
20
), and (
22
), we used the fact that the tensor product of irreducible indecomposable highest-weight representations decomposes into a direct sum in the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
in the same way as in the case of the classical algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. However, when the deformation parameter
q
of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
is equal to a root of unity, this is no longer true. In the classical algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, there is an infinite number of irreducible representations. In contrast, when
q
is equal to a root of unity, the number of irreducible indecomposable highest-weight representations in the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
becomes finite. The higher the order of the root of unity of the parameter
q
, the more irreducible indecomposable highest-weight representations can be constructed. This fact will be discussed in more detail in Section
2
.

It was shown
[
21
,
34
]
that for
SU(N)_{k}
Chern–Simons theory the deformation parameter of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
must be equal to

q=\exp\left(\frac{\pi i}{k+N}\right).

(27)

Moreover, it is stated that Ising anyons correspond to
SU(2)_{2}
Chern–Simons theory
[
26
,
28
,
27
]
. Thus, in the context of this paper,
q
is a root of unity. The order
\operatorname{ord}(q)
of the root of unity
q
is equal to 8. Therefore, in order to compute formulas of the form (
20
) and (
26
), it is necessary to understand the rules for decomposing tensor products of irreducible indecomposable highest-weight representations into direct sums at roots of unity, since these rules directly affect the computations in (
20
) and (
26
).

2
Highest weight representations of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
in roots of unity

Recall that Ising anyons are stated to correspond to
SU(2)_{2}
Chern–Simons theory. In the context of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, this leads to the consideration of representations at roots of unity for computing the expectation values of Wilson loops using formulas (
20
) and (
26
).

Let us begin with some general facts about the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})

[
18
]
. The quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
is generated by the operators
K
,
K^{-1}
,
E
, and
F
, subject to the commutation relations

\displaystyle KEK^{-1}=q^{2}E,\qquad KFK^{-1}=q^{-2}F,

(28)

\displaystyle[E,F]=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}

(29)

or, equivalently, by the operators
E
,
F
, and
H
, subject to the commutation relations

\begin{split}[H;E]=2&E,\quad[H;F]=-2F,\\
[E;F]&=\frac{e^{hH}-e^{-hH}}{e^{h}-e^{-h}}.\end{split}

(30)

The first case is often denoted by
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, while the second is denoted by
U_{h}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. Since the following correspondence holds,

q=e^{h},\quad K=e^{hH}

(31)

in this work, the notation
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
will be used for both cases.

To define the action of the quantum algebra on the tensor square of representations, it is necessary to introduce the coproduct

\displaystyle\Delta(E)=E\otimes K+1\otimes E,\qquad\Delta(F)=F\otimes 1+K^{-1}\otimes F,

(32)

\displaystyle\Delta(H)=H\otimes 1+1\otimes H,\qquad\Delta(K)=K\otimes K.

(33)

Let us note an important property of the coproduct. It commutes with the
\mathcal{R}
-matrix (
13
). This is precisely why the vectors in the decomposition of a tensor product of representations into a direct sum are eigenvectors of the
\mathcal{R}
-matrix.

Let us consider finite-dimensional representations of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. When
q
is not a root of unity, all finite-dimensional representations are highest-weight representations, just as in the classical case. However, when
q
becomes a root of unity, a much broader class of representations arises. In addition to highest-weight representations (spin), one also obtains cyclic representations, semicyclic representations, representations with free highest weight, as well as reducible but indecomposable representations (ind)
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
. In the context of the present work, we are primarily interested in the usual highest-weight representations of spin type and in the highest-weight representations of ind type, since, as will be shown below, the tensor product of representations of spin type may decompose into representations of ind type.

2.1
Spin type representations

v_{0}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
Ev_{1}=[\gamma]_{q}v_{0}
Fv_{0}=v_{1}
Ev_{2}=[2]_{q}[\gamma-1]_{q}v_{1}
Fv_{1}=v_{2}
Ev_{3}=[3]_{q}[\gamma-2]_{q}v_{2}
Fv_{2}=v_{3}
Hv_{0}=\gamma v_{0}
Hv_{1}=(\gamma-2)v_{1}
Hv_{2}=(\gamma-4)v_{2}
Hv_{3}=(\gamma-6)v_{3}
\vdots

Figure 4:
A highest-weight representation of the quantum algebra
U_{h}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. The relations to the left of the diagram are postulated, while those to the right are derived from the postulates.

Let us consider the following construction of a finite-dimensional irreducible highest-weight representation (Fig.
4
). We postulate the action of the raising operator
E
and the Cartan operator
H
on the highest-weight vector
v_{0}
by

Ev_{0}=0,\quad Hv_{0}=\gamma v_{0}

(34)

respectively. Here,
\gamma
is the highest weight. We also define the action of the lowering operator by

Fv_{n}=v_{n+1}.

(35)

Using the commutation relations (
30
), one can derive

\displaystyle Hv_{n}=(\gamma-2n)v_{n},

(36)

\displaystyle\qquad Ev_{n}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n-1}[\gamma-2k]_{q}v_{n-1}=[n]_{q}[\gamma-(n-1)]_{q}v_{n-1}

(37)

where the so-called quantum numbers are used, defined as follows

[p]_{q}\equiv\frac{q^{p}-q^{-p}}{q-q^{-1}}.

(38)

Thus, one obtains the construction shown in Fig.
4
. For ease of presentation, in Fig.
4
the relations on the left are postulated, whereas those on the right are derived. This construction yields finite-dimensional irreducible highest-weight representations of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma/2,+)
. The meaning of the sign
+
in the notation of the representation will become clear somewhat later.

Since we are analyzing representations at roots of unity (that is, with
q
a root of unity), let
m
be a minimal positive integer such that
q^{m}=1
. Namely,
m=\operatorname{ord}(q)
. Then
m^{\prime}
is defined as follows:

m^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{aligned} m&\qquad\text{, if $m$ is odd}\\
m/2&\qquad\text{, if $m$ is even}\end{aligned}\right.

(39)

In the context of the present work, the number
m^{\prime}
is important because it is the smallest positive integer for which
\operatorname{Im}(q^{m^{\prime}})=0
. Thus,
m^{\prime}
is also the minimal number for which

[m^{\prime}]_{q}=0.

(40)

This number will also play an important role in the next section. In addition, a finite-dimensional irreducible highest-weight representation of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma/2,+)
of dimension
d
can be obtained from the condition

\gamma=d-1

(41)

However, it is important that the dimension
d
not exceed the value of
m^{\prime}
, since otherwise the finite-dimensional indecomposable highest-weight representation becomes reducible. This would contradict the purpose of the construction shown in Fig.
4
, namely, to obtain finite-dimensional irreducible highest-weight representations.

In addition to the representations derived from the construction in Fig.
4
, there also exist representations of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma,-)
. The differences between these two types of representations are as follows:

\displaystyle\text{spin}(\gamma/2,+)\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad\text{spin}(\gamma/2,-)

(42)

\displaystyle Kv_{0}=q^{\gamma}v_{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad Kv_{0}=-q^{\gamma}v_{0}

(43)

In general, a representation of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma/2,-)
cannot be transformed into a representation of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma/2,+)
by a change of basis, nor even by replacing
q
with another root of unity of the same order. However, it is worth noting that, in the case where
m
is even, the following relation holds:

-Kv_{0}=-q^{\gamma}v_{0}=q^{\gamma+m/2}v_{0}=q^{\gamma+m^{\prime}}v_{0},

(44)

in contrast to the case where
m
is odd

q^{\gamma+m^{\prime}}v_{0}=q^{\gamma+m}v_{0}=q^{\gamma}v_{0}=Kv_{0}.

(45)

As a consequence, whenever
m
is even, the tensor product of two representations of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma/2,+)
and
\mathrm{ind}(\gamma^{\prime}/2,+)
may, upon decomposition into a direct sum, also produce representations of the form
\mathrm{ind}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}/2,-)
. The latter denotes a reducible but indecomposable representation in which one can isolate a subrepresentation of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}/2,-)
. These representations will be discussed in greater detail in the next section. It is also worth noting that there is the following way to obtain a representation of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma/2,-)
:

\text{spin}(\gamma/2,-)=\text{spin}(\gamma/2,+)\otimes\text{spin}(0,-).

(46)

For representations of the spin type with classical dimension
d
, it is natural to introduce the quantum dimension
\mathfrak{d}=[d]_{q}
. The quantum dimension arises from the need to compute the quantum trace over all possible vectors. For this reason, the quantum dimension
\mathfrak{d}
is equal to the trace of the operator
H
. In addition to being important for formula (
20
), the quantum dimension also reproduces the decomposition into a direct sum. An example of this fact can be seen in formula (
52
).

2.2
Ind type representations

As already mentioned, in addition to irreducible finite-dimensional highest-weight representations, the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
also possesses reducible but indecomposable representations of the form
\mathrm{ind}(\gamma/2,\pm)
. Each of them has classical dimension
2m^{\prime}
.The quantum dimension for representations of the
\operatorname{ind}
type can also be introduced as the trace of the operator
H
. For representations of the
\operatorname{ind}
type, the quantum dimension is always equal to zero and consists of the sum of two quantum numbers, as, for example, in formula (
50
). Moreover, the quantum dimension for representations of the
\operatorname{ind}
type satisfies the decomposition property for the tensor product of the corresponding representations into a direct sum.

The notation
\mathrm{ind}(\gamma/2,\pm)
means that such a representation contains a distinguished irreducible part of the form
\mathrm{spin}(\gamma/2,\pm)
. In the context of the present work, representations of ind type are important because they arise in the decomposition of tensor products of the form
\mathrm{spin}\otimes\mathrm{spin}
.

For clarity, let us present a simple yet illustrative example of an ind representation. For this example, we use the tensor product representation

\text{spin}(1/2,+)\otimes\text{spin}(1/2,+)

(47)

of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at
q=i
(Fig.
5
). This value of
q
is used only in the present subsection and is chosen solely to simplify the example.

\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle+i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle
\mid\uparrow\uparrow\rangle
\mid\downarrow\downarrow\rangle
\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle-i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle
\Delta(E)(\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle+i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle)=2i\mid\uparrow\uparrow\rangle
\Delta(F)\mid\uparrow\uparrow\rangle=\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle-i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle
\Delta(F)(\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle+i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle)=2i\mid\downarrow\downarrow\rangle
\Delta(E)\mid\downarrow\downarrow\rangle=\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle-i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle

Figure 5:
structure of
\text{spin}(1/2+)\otimes\text{spin}(1/2,+)
at
q=i
.

Let us note that the action of the raising and lowering operators on one of the vectors is zero:

\Delta(E)(\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle-i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle)=0=\Delta(F)(\mid\downarrow\uparrow\rangle-i\mid\uparrow\downarrow\rangle).

(48)

This means that one can isolate a subrepresentation of the form
\mathrm{spin}(0,+)
inside this representation. However, it is not possible to decompose this representation into a direct sum. Thus, we obtain a reducible but indecomposable representation
\mathrm{ind}(0,+)
. That is,

\text{spin}(1/2,+)\otimes\text{spin}(1/2,+)=\text{ind}(0,+).

(49)

Let us also note that the classical dimension of the representation
\mathrm{ind}(0,+)
is equal to
2m^{\prime}=2\cdot 2=4
. It is then natural to compute the quantum dimension of this representation:

[2]_{q}*[2]_{q}=[3]_{q}+[1]_{q}=0.

(50)

The last formula reflects several interesting facts:

1.

the quantum dimension of this ind representation is zero;

2.

this ind representation is formed by the ”gluing” of two representations:
\mathrm{spin}(1)
and
\mathrm{spin}(0)
.

These two facts are not accidental. Ind representations are always formed from the sum of two representations in such a way that the sum of their classical dimensions is equal to
2m^{\prime}
, while the quantum dimension is zero.

3
Representations of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
in the root of unity
q=\exp(\pi i/4)

As already mentioned above, Ising anyons are claimed to correspond to
SU(2)_{2}
Chern–Simons theory. Observables in Chern–Simons theory can be computed using the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. Formula (
27
) relates the parameters
N
and
k
in
SU(N)_{k}
Chern–Simons theory to the deformation parameter
q
of the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. Thus, one expects that in the quantum algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at
q=\exp(\pi i/4)
, the formulas (
3
), (
4
), and (
8
), which are key for the minimal model
\mathcal{M}(4,3)
, should appear. Next, in this section, we will obtain rules analogous to (
3
) and (
4
). Representations which have zero quantum dimension will disappear from the calculations according to the (
20
). Therefore relations (
3
) and (
4
) indeed hold.

Using the construction described above in Section
2
(Fig.
4
), one can construct the following irreducible highest-weight representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at
q=\exp(\pi i/4)
:

\varnothing

spin(0,
\pm
)

\mathfrak{d}_{1}=[1]_{q}=1

d_{1}=1
;

spin(1/2,
\pm
)

\mathfrak{d}_{2}=[2]_{q}=\sqrt{2}

d_{2}=2
;

spin(1,
\pm
)

\mathfrak{d}_{3}=[3]_{q}=1

d_{3}=3
;

spin(3/2,
\pm
)

\mathfrak{d}_{4}=[4]_{q}=0

d_{4}=4
;

where
\mathfrak{d}
denotes the quantum dimension, while
d
denotes the classical dimension of the spin representation. Their tensor products generate reducible but indecomposable representations

ind(0,
\pm
)

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{1}=[1]_{q}+[7]_{q}=0

d_{1}^{\prime}=8
;

ind(1/2,
\pm
)

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{2}=[2]_{q}+[6]_{q}=0

d_{2}^{\prime}=8
;

ind(1,
\pm
)

\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{3}=[3]_{q}+[5]_{q}=0

d_{3}^{\prime}=8
;

where
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
denotes the quantum dimension, while
d^{\prime}
denotes the classical dimension of the ind representation.

Let us consider the multiplication table
\text{spin}\otimes\text{spin}
:

spin(0,+)

spin(1/2,+)

spin(1,+)

spin(3/2,+)

spin(0,+)

spin(0,+)

spin(1/2,+)

spin(1,+)

spin(3/2,+)

spin(1/2,+)

spin(1/2,+)

spin(1,+)
\oplus
spin(0,+)

spin(3/2,+)
\oplus
spin(1/2,+)

Ind(1,+)

spin(1,+)

spin(1,+)

spin(3/2,+)
\oplus
spin(1/2,+)

Ind(1,+)
\oplus
spin(0,+)

Ind(1/2,+)
\oplus
spin(3/2,+)

spin(3/2,+)

spin(3/2,+)

Ind(1,+)

Ind(1/2,+)
\oplus
spin(3/2,+)

Ind(0,+)
\oplus
Ind(1,+)

Based on this table, it is natural to introduce the following correspondence between Ising anyons and representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at
q=\exp(\pi i/4)
:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\text{spin}(0,+)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathfrak{d}_{\mathbbm{1}}\equiv\mathfrak{d}_{1}

\displaystyle=1;

(51)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\text{spin}(1/2,+)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}\equiv\mathfrak{d}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2};

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\text{spin}(1,+)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathfrak{d}_{\psi}\equiv\mathfrak{d}_{3}

\displaystyle=1;

where the notation
\mathfrak{d}_{\mathbbm{1}}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}
, and
\mathfrak{d}_{\psi}
is introduced solely to simplify the demonstration of property (
52
). In this case, the fusion rules for Ising anyons (
3
) are reproduced up to representations whose quantum dimension is zero. Formula (
20
) allows us to understand why we can exclude from the computations precisely those representations whose quantum dimension is equal to zero, namely
\operatorname{spin}(3/2,+)
and all representations of the
\operatorname{ind}
type. Let us also note that an analogue of formula (
3
) holds for the quantum dimensions:

\begin{split}\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}&=\mathfrak{d}_{\mathbbm{1}}+\mathfrak{d}_{\psi}\\
\mathfrak{d}_{\psi}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{\sigma}&=\mathfrak{d}_{\psi}\\
\mathfrak{d}_{\psi}\cdot\mathfrak{d}_{\psi}&=\mathfrak{d}_{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\end{split}

(52)

The correspondence (
51
) also reproduces the braiding rules (
4
). Braiding rules for two representations are obtained by
\mathcal{R}
-matrix

\mathcal{R}=q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n(n+1)/2}(1-q^{-2})^{n}}{[n]_{q}!}E^{n}\otimes F^{n}

(53)

In the present case, the braiding rules for the representations
\mathrm{spin}(1/2,+)\otimes\mathrm{spin}(1/2,+)
, are given by

\mathcal{R}=\begin{pmatrix}R_{\text{spin}(1/2,+)\otimes\text{spin(1/2,+)}}^{\text{spin}(0,+)}&0\\
0&R_{\text{spin}(1/2,+)\otimes\text{spin(1/2,+)}}^{\text{spin}(1,+)}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-q^{-3/2}&0\\
0&q^{1/2}\end{pmatrix}=ie^{i\pi/8}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&e^{-i\pi/2}\end{pmatrix}

(54)

Thus, the correspondence (
51
) reproduces the fusion rules (
3
) and the braiding rules (
4
) up to an overall phase factor, which does not affect the probability of the observable. Among the key properties of Ising anyons, it remains only to verify the validity of (
8
). To do this, one has to consider
\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}
.

Figure 6:
Defenition of fusion matrix F

Such an arbitrariness in the choice of decomposition order gives rise, in the present case, to two bases, which are as previously conventionally represented by tree diagrams (such as on Fig.
6
). The fusion of two branches determines the order in which the corresponding tensor product of representations is decomposed into a direct sum. Of particular interest here is the transition matrix between the highest-weight vectors, since the full transition matrix between the bases can then be easily reconstructed by taking tensor product of each element with the identity matrix. The size of this identity matrix is determined by the dimension of the corresponding representation. By the fusion matrix, one precisely means the transition matrix between the highest-weight vectors of those representations whose decompositions yield isomorphic representations. For example, there is a fusion matrix

(59)

Highest-weight vectors are defined up to multiplication by an arbitrary scalar. In the case of normalized vectors, they are defined up to a phase factor. Thus, the fusion matrix takes the form

\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{0}\end{pmatrix}=F\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{[2]_{q}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\sqrt{[3]_{q}}\\
\sqrt{[3]_{q}}&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{0}\end{pmatrix}

(60)

and is defined up to left and right multiplication by a diagonal matrix of the form

\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\phi_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{i\phi_{2}}\end{pmatrix},

(61)

where
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are some real phases.

(62)

Formula (
60
) is valid when
q
is not a root of unity, or when the order
m
of the root is sufficiently large. In our case, the latter condition is satisfied. The order
m
is large enough for the decomposition

\mathrm{spin}(1/2,+)\otimes\mathrm{spin}(1/2,+)\otimes\mathrm{spin}(1/2,+)=\mathrm{spin}(3/2,+)\oplus 2\,\mathrm{spin}(1/2,+)

(63)

at
q=\exp(i\pi/4)
to coincide with the decomposition

(64)

for
q
not equal to a root of unity. When
q
is a root of unity, however, this is not always the case, since the number of highest-weight representations is bounded. The origin of this restriction lies precisely in the fact that
q
is a root of unity.

Since the decomposition (
63
) coincides with the decomposition (
64
), the fusion matrix (
60
) admits a well-defined limit. Substituting
q=\exp(i\pi/4)
into the fusion matrix (
60
), we obtain

\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{0}\end{pmatrix}=F\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{0}\end{pmatrix}

(65)

which coincides with the fusion matrix for Ising anyons (
8
). The same matrix can also be obtained directly by substituting
q=\exp(i\pi/4)
into the formulas for the highest-weight vectors (
58
) and (
59
), and then computing the transition matrix between them.

. However, this matrix is not relevant for us in the context of Ising anyons, since the representation

Thus, we have reproduced all three key formulas for Ising anyons: (
3
), (
4
), and (
8
). One could stop at this point; however, the study of tensor products of degree greater than three is of some interest. The tensor product

between the bases shown in Fig.

.

According to (
66
) the matrix
F^{\tiny\;\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.40831pt\vbox{\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower height=4.15143pt,width=0.40831pt}\kern-0.20415pt\kern-4.15143pt\hrule width=4.55974pt,height=0.40831pt\kern 3.74312pt\hrule width=4.55974pt,height=0.40831pt}&\lower 0.40831pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.20415pt\hbox{\kern 0.40831pt\vbox to4.15143pt{\vss\hbox to3.74312pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-4.15143pt\vrule height=4.15143pt,width=0.40831pt\kern 3.74312pt\vrule height=4.15143pt,width=0.40831pt}\kern-0.20415pt\kern-4.15143pt\hrule width=4.55974pt,height=0.40831pt\kern 3.74312pt\hrule width=4.55974pt,height=0.40831pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}
has size
3\times 3
. It takes the following form as a function of
q
:

\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{[3]_{q}}}&-\frac{\sqrt{[4]_{q}}}{\sqrt{[2]_{q}[3]_{q}}}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{[3]_{q}}}&-\frac{[4]_{q}}{[3]_{q}[2]_{q}}&-\frac{\sqrt{[4]_{q}}}{[3]_{q}\sqrt{[2]_{q}}}\\
-\frac{\sqrt{[4]_{q}}}{\sqrt{[2]_{q}[3]_{q}}}&-\frac{\sqrt{[4]_{q}}}{[3]_{q}\sqrt{[q]_{2}}}&-\frac{1}{[3]_{q}}\end{pmatrix}

(67)

This matrix can be obtained directly by computing the highest vectors, in analogy with what was done in formulas (
58
) and (
59
).

Let us note, however, that the representations shown in Fig.
9
contain subrepresentations
\mathrm{spin}(3/2,+)
. When
q
becomes equal to
\exp(i\pi/4)
, the quantum dimension of the representation
\mathrm{spin}(3/2,+)
becomes zero. Therefore, according to (
20
)the contribution of such a representation must vanish. Moreover, the contribution of the representation shown in Fig.
9
must also vanish for the same reasons. All this leads to the fact that the left representations in Figs.
9
and
9
must glue together, forming the representation
\mathrm{ind}(1,+)
, whose quantum dimension is zero, as must the right representations in the same figures. Thus, the representations shown in Fig.
9
and
9
indeed do not contribute to the observable quantity. Therefore, at
q=\exp(i\pi/4)
, one has the decomposition

\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}=\text{ind}(1,+)\oplus 2\;\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}&\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}\oplus 2\varnothing.

(68)

This decomposition can also be reproduced directly by writing out all the vectors and the actions of the quantum-algebra operators on them.

At
q\to\exp(i\pi/4)
, the matrix (
67
) becomes

\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(69)

Although the matrix (
67
) was obtained using the fact that the decomposition (
68
) is no longer valid, it satisfies some expected requirements. Namely, it becomes a block-diagonal matrix: a
2\times 2
block appears in it. The remaining
1\times 1
component corresponds to the vector that became part of an
\operatorname{ind}
representation. Guided by the results of the Ising minimal model, it is natural to expect that the transition matrix such as on Fig.
9
should have size
2\times 2
. However, direct calculations show that this is not the case. This is a rather unexpected and interesting result.

(73)

ceased to be the highest vector of an irreducible spin representation and became part of an
\operatorname{ind}
representation. For this reason, in the subsequent computations, one has to keep the vector (
73
), even though it has become part of an
\operatorname{ind}
representation. Without this vector, the transition between the bases becomes impossible. Thus, an apparent contradiction arises: it seems as if a representation with quantum dimension
0
contributes to the observable quantity. It will be shown below that this is not the case.

Thus, due to the decomposition
68
, the limiting transition for the matrix (
69
) does not exist. Let us compute directly the transition matrices between the following bases:

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 27.85 8.78 h 50.61 v 17.87 h -50.61 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{23.45758pt}{9.67865pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(74)

Where the matrices
\hat{A}
and
\hat{C}
are equal to

\hat{A}=\hat{C}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{F}&0&0\\
0&\hat{F}&0\\
0&0&1_{2\times 2}\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad\hat{F}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\
1&1\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(75)

And the matrix
\hat{B}
is equal to

\hat{B}=\begin{pmatrix}1_{3\times 3}&0&0&0\\
0&-1&-\sqrt{1-i}&0\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(76)

All three matrices satisfy the property

\hat{A}^{2}=\hat{B}^{2}=\hat{C}^{2}=Id

(77)

All bases in formula (
74
) were constructed so that the diagonal
\mathcal{R}
-matrix in any of the bases is equal to

\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}=\text{diag}(-q^{-1},q,-q^{-1},q,q,q).

(78)

Then, for the basis
W

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{W,1}&=\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\\
\mathcal{R}_{W,2}&=\hat{A}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{A}\\
\mathcal{R}_{W,3}&=\hat{A}\hat{B}\hat{C}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{C}\hat{B}\hat{A}\end{split},

(79)

for the basis
V

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{V,1}&=\hat{A}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{A}\\
\mathcal{R}_{V,2}&=\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\\
\mathcal{R}_{V,3}&=\hat{B}\hat{C}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{C}\hat{B}\end{split}

(80)

for the basis
X

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{X,1}&=\hat{B}\hat{A}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{A}\hat{B}\\
\mathcal{R}_{X,2}&=\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\\
\mathcal{R}_{X,3}&=\hat{C}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{C}\end{split},

(81)

and for the basis
K

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{K,1}&=\hat{C}\hat{B}\hat{A}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{A}\hat{B}\hat{C}\\
\mathcal{R}_{K,2}&=\hat{C}\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\hat{C}\\
\mathcal{R}_{K,3}&=\mathcal{R}_{\text{diag}}\end{split}.

(82)

For any of these bases, the braid group relations are satisfied:

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{1}&=\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{2}\\
\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{3}\mathcal{R}_{2}&=\mathcal{R}_{3}\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathcal{R}_{3}\\
\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathcal{R}_{3}&=\mathcal{R}_{3}\mathcal{R}_{1}\\
\end{split}

(83)

This suggests that all the contradictions that arise are in fact only apparent and do not affect the computations. Indeed, let us take the probability matrix for a system of 6 vectors to be in a given state:

\hat{P}=\text{diag}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\theta,\theta)

(84)

and the matrix of quantum dimensions

\hat{Q}=\text{diag}(1,1,1,1,\xi,-\xi).

(85)

In the matrix
\hat{P}
, the last two probabilities
\theta
coincide, since they belong to two vectors in the same representation
\operatorname{ind}(1,+)
. All other probabilities belong to representations of the spin type. In the quantum-dimension matrix
\hat{Q}
, the last two quantum dimensions
\xi
and
-\xi
belong to two former representations of the spin type that merged into an
\operatorname{ind}
representation; therefore, the sum of their quantum dimensions must be equal to zero. It turns out that for any sequence of
\mathcal{R}
-matrices
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{any word}}
, the expression

\text{Tr}(\hat{Q}\mathcal{R}_{\text{any word}}\hat{P})=\text{func}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta),

(86)

which corresponds to formula (
20
), depends only on the probabilities
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
, and
\delta
of the representations of the spin type. Moreover, statement (
86
) remains true even if in the matrix (
76
) one replaces
-\sqrt{1-i}
by an arbitrary parameter
\epsilon
. However, statement (
86
) is violated if one introduces

\hat{P^{\prime}}=\text{diag}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})

(87)

and

\hat{Q}=\text{diag}(1,1,1,1,\xi_{1},-\xi_{2}).

(88)

such that

\theta_{1}\xi_{1}-\theta_{2}\xi_{2}\neq 0.

(89)

However, if
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
and
\xi_{1}
,
\xi_{2}
belong to the same
\operatorname{ind}
representation, then
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=\theta
and
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi
. Then
\theta_{1}\xi_{1}-\theta_{2}\xi_{2}=0.

Thus, despite the unexpected form of the matrix (
76
) and the fact that the highest vectors corresponding to spin representations ceased to be expressible in terms of one another, the probability (
20
) will depend only on spin representations. It follows that the tensor product
\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}
in
SU(2)_{2}
Chern–Simons theory contains no contradictions with Ising anyons.

6
Conclusion

It is known that the expectation values of Wilson loops in
SU(2)_{k}
Chern–Simons theory can be computed using the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction, since the expectation values of Wilson loops are expressed in terms of Jones polynomials. In this case, the computation of observables is based on the representation theory of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at roots of unity.

On the other hand, a constant-time slice in
(2+1)
-dimensional spacetime intersects the integration contour of a Wilson loop at certain points. The correlators at these points coincide with the correlators of some conformal field theory.

It is well known that
SU(2)_{2}
Chern–Simons theory should correspond to Ising anyons, that is, to the minimal model
\mathcal{M}(4,3)
of conformal field theory. Thus, the key relations for Ising anyons, (
3
), (
4
), and (
8
), should in some way be reproduced in the representation theory of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
at
q=\exp(i\pi/4)
. This is precisely the subject of the present paper. In Section
3
, relations (
3
) and (
4
) were reproduced. In Section
4
, relation (
8
) was reproduced. Section
5
is of interest from the point of view of representation theory, since in the decomposition of the tensor product
\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}\otimes\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.59998pt\vbox{\kern 0.29999pt\hbox{\kern 0.59998pt\vbox to6.1pt{\vss\hbox to5.50003pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-6.1pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\vrule height=6.1pt,width=0.59998pt}\kern-0.29999pt\kern-6.1pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt\kern 5.50003pt\hrule width=6.69998pt,height=0.59998pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}
, representations whose structure differs from that of classical irreducible representations appear for the first time. It was explained that all contradictions appearing in this tensor product are only apparent. These results are du to the fact that the representations with zero quantum dimension disappear form the observables. In section
3
fusion rules are reproduced for everything except for the representations which have zro quantum dimension. In section
5
the zero quantum dimension representaions also do not contribute to observable (
86
).

7
Futher investigations

7.1
qP symmetry

One may observe that there is a simple way to obtain the highest-weight vectors of the space (
58
) from the highest-weight vectors of the space (
59
). It is sufficient to replace
q
by
q^{-1}
(the
q
-symmetry) and to reflect all “spins” completely with respect to the midpoint, for example,

\mid\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\to\mid\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow\rangle

(the
P
-symmetry).

7.2
Tensor products of higher degree

In Section
5
, we explained why all the contradictions that arise and do not coincide with expectations turn out to be only apparent contradictions. It is also useful to trace that, in the decomposition of tensor products of higher degree, representations with quantum dimension
0
do not contribute to the computations.

7.3
A general formula for the decomposition of tensor products of the form
\text{spin}\otimes\text{ind}

One possible direction for further research is to find a general formula for the decomposition of tensor products of the form
\text{spin}\otimes\text{ind}
into a direct sum for
q
equal to an arbitrary root of unity.

7.4
Studying the properties of tensor products of the form
\text{ind}\otimes\text{ind}
and finding a general decomposition formula

It is also useful to study the decomposition into a direct sum of tensor products of the form
\text{ind}\otimes\text{ind}
.

Ind(0,+)

Ind(1/2,+)

Ind(1,+)

Ind(0,+)

4 Ind(1,+)
\oplus
2 Ind(1,-)
\oplus
2 Ind(0,+)

4 spin(3/2,+)
\oplus
2 Ind(1/2,-)
\oplus
4 spin(3/2,-)
\oplus

2 Ind(1,-)
\oplus
2 Ind(0,+)
\oplus
4 Ind(1,+)

Table 1:
\text{ind}\otimes\text{ind}
decomposition table.

Let us pay attention to Table
1
. One can notice an unusual and interesting property. In addition to being symmetric with respect to the main diagonal, the table is also symmetric with respect to the secondary diagonal. It would be useful to understand the nature of this property, to try to find other interesting properties, if any, and to derive a general formula for the decomposition into a direct sum of tensor products of the form
\text{ind}\otimes\text{ind}
for
q
equal to an arbitrary root of unity.
